</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Bernard of Clairvaux</b>, <a href="/wiki/Cistercians" title="Cistercians">O. Cist.</a> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Bernardus Claraevallensis</i>; 1090&#160;&#8211;&#32;20 August 1153), venerated as <b>Saint Bernard</b>, was an <a href="/wiki/Abbot" title="Abbot">abbot</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystic</a>, co-founder of the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Templars" class="mw-redirect" title="Knights Templars">Knights Templars</a>, and a major leader in the reformation of the <a href="/wiki/Benedictines" title="Benedictines">Benedictine Order</a> through the nascent <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Cistercians" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Cistercians">Cistercian Order</a>.
</p><p>He was sent to found <a href="/wiki/Clairvaux_Abbey" title="Clairvaux Abbey">Clairvaux Abbey</a> at an isolated clearing in a glen known as the <i>Val d'Absinthe</i>, about 15 kilometres (9&#160;mi) southeast of <a href="/wiki/Bar-sur-Aube" title="Bar-sur-Aube">Bar-sur-Aube</a>. In the year 1128, Bernard attended the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Troyes_1129" class="mw-redirect" title="Council of Troyes 1129">Council of Troyes</a>, at which he traced the outlines of the Rule of the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Templar" title="Knights Templar">Knights Templar</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> which soon became an ideal of Christian nobility.
</p><p>On the death of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Honorius_II" title="Pope Honorius II">Pope Honorius II</a> in 1130, a schism arose in the church. Bernard was a major proponent of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_II" title="Pope Innocent II">Pope Innocent II</a>, arguing effectively for his legitimacy over the <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Anacletus_II" title="Antipope Anacletus II">Antipope Anacletus II</a>.
</p><p>In 1139, Bernard attended the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_the_Lateran" title="Second Council of the Lateran">Second Council of the Lateran</a> and criticized <a href="/wiki/Peter_Abelard" title="Peter Abelard">Peter Abelard</a> vocally. Bernard advocated crusades in general and convinced many to participate in the unsuccessful <a href="/wiki/Second_Crusade" title="Second Crusade">Second Crusade</a>, notably through a famous sermon at <a href="/wiki/Council_of_V%C3%A9zelay_(1146)" class="mw-redirect" title="Council of Vézelay (1146)">Vézelay (1146)</a>.
</p><p>Bernard was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> just 21 years after his death by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_III" title="Pope Alexander III">Pope Alexander III</a>. In 1830 <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_VIII" title="Pope Pius VIII">Pope Pius VIII</a> declared him a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life_.281090.E2.80.931113.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_(1090–1113)">Early life (1090–1113)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life (1090–1113)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bernard's parents were <a href="/wiki/Tescelin_de_Fontaine" class="mw-redirect" title="Tescelin de Fontaine">Tescelin de Fontaine</a>, lord of <a href="/wiki/Fontaine-l%C3%A8s-Dijon" title="Fontaine-lès-Dijon">Fontaine-lès-Dijon</a>, and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Al%C3%A8the_de_Montbard&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Alèthe de Montbard (page does not exist)">Alèthe de Montbard</a><sup class="noprint" style="font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al%C3%A8the_(sainte)" class="extiw" title="fr:Alèthe (sainte)">fr</a>&#93;</sup>, both members of the highest nobility of <a href="/wiki/Burgundy_(region)" class="mw-redirect" title="Burgundy (region)">Burgundy</a>. Bernard was the third of seven children, six of whom were sons. Aged nine, he was sent to a school at <a href="/wiki/Ch%C3%A2tillon-sur-Seine" title="Châtillon-sur-Seine">Châtillon-sur-Seine</a> run by the <a href="/wiki/Secular_canons" class="mw-redirect" title="Secular canons">secular canons</a> of Saint-Vorles. Bernard had an interest in literature and rhetoric. He had a special devotion to the <a href="/wiki/Blessed_Virgin_Mary_(Roman_Catholic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Blessed Virgin Mary (Roman Catholic)">Virgin Mary</a>, and he later wrote several works about the <a href="/wiki/Queen_of_Heaven" title="Queen of Heaven">Queen of Heaven</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Fra_bartolomeo_02_Vision_of_St_Bernard_with_Sts_Benedict_and_John_the_Evangelist.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Fra_bartolomeo_02_Vision_of_St_Bernard_with_Sts_Benedict_and_John_the_Evangelist.jpg/220px-Fra_bartolomeo_02_Vision_of_St_Bernard_with_Sts_Benedict_and_John_the_Evangelist.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="212" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Fra_bartolomeo_02_Vision_of_St_Bernard_with_Sts_Benedict_and_John_the_Evangelist.jpg/330px-Fra_bartolomeo_02_Vision_of_St_Bernard_with_Sts_Benedict_and_John_the_Evangelist.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Fra_bartolomeo_02_Vision_of_St_Bernard_with_Sts_Benedict_and_John_the_Evangelist.jpg/440px-Fra_bartolomeo_02_Vision_of_St_Bernard_with_Sts_Benedict_and_John_the_Evangelist.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1065" data-file-height="1027" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Fra_bartolomeo_02_Vision_of_St_Bernard_with_Sts_Benedict_and_John_the_Evangelist.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>The Vision of St Bernard</i>, by <a href="/wiki/Fra_Bartolommeo" class="mw-redirect" title="Fra Bartolommeo">Fra Bartolommeo</a>, c. 1504 (<a href="/wiki/Uffizi" title="Uffizi">Uffizi</a>)</div></div></div>
<p>Bernard emphasized the value of a personally held faith, with the life of Christ as a model and new emphasis on the Virgin Mary. In opposition to the rational approach to divine understanding used by the <a href="/wiki/Scholastics" class="mw-redirect" title="Scholastics">scholastics</a>, Bernard preached an immediate faith, in which the intercessor was the Virgin Mary.
</p><p>Bernard was nineteen years old when his mother died. During his youth, he did not escape trying temptations and around this time he thought of living a life of solitude and prayer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998129_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998129-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1098, a group led by <a href="/wiki/Robert_of_Molesme" title="Robert of Molesme">Robert of Molesme</a> had founded <a href="/wiki/C%C3%AEteaux_Abbey" title="Cîteaux Abbey">Cîteaux Abbey</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Dijon" title="Dijon">Dijon</a>, with the purpose of living literally according to the <a href="/wiki/Rule_of_St_Benedict" class="mw-redirect" title="Rule of St Benedict">Rule of St Benedict</a>. After his mother died, Bernard decided to go to Cîteaux. In 1113 he and thirty other young noblemen of Burgundy sought admission into the new monastery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcManners1990204_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcManners1990204-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Bernard's example was so convincing that scores followed him into the monastic life.
</p>
<h2><span id="Abbot_of_Clairvaux_.281115.E2.80.9328.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Abbot_of_Clairvaux_(1115–28)">Abbot of Clairvaux (1115–28)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Abbot of Clairvaux (1115–28)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Jorg_Breu_Sr_St_Bernhard_Zwettl.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/2/28/Jorg_Breu_Sr_St_Bernhard_Zwettl.jpg/220px-Jorg_Breu_Sr_St_Bernhard_Zwettl.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="266" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/2/28/Jorg_Breu_Sr_St_Bernhard_Zwettl.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="306" data-file-height="370" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Jorg_Breu_Sr_St_Bernhard_Zwettl.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Bernard exorcising a possession, altarpiece by <a href="/wiki/J%C3%B6rg_Breu_the_Elder" title="Jörg Breu the Elder">Jörg Breu the Elder</a>, c. 1500</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Marcello_Baschenis,_San_Bernardo_e_Satana,_1885.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Marcello_Baschenis%2C_San_Bernardo_e_Satana%2C_1885.jpg/220px-Marcello_Baschenis%2C_San_Bernardo_e_Satana%2C_1885.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="301" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Marcello_Baschenis%2C_San_Bernardo_e_Satana%2C_1885.jpg/330px-Marcello_Baschenis%2C_San_Bernardo_e_Satana%2C_1885.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Marcello_Baschenis%2C_San_Bernardo_e_Satana%2C_1885.jpg/440px-Marcello_Baschenis%2C_San_Bernardo_e_Satana%2C_1885.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="1093" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Marcello_Baschenis,_San_Bernardo_e_Satana,_1885.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Bernard holding a demon at his feet, oiloncanvas by <a href="/wiki/Marcello_Baschenis" title="Marcello Baschenis">Marcello Baschenis</a>, c. 1885</div></div></div>
<p>The little community of reformed <a href="/wiki/Benedictines" title="Benedictines">Benedictines</a> at <a href="/wiki/C%C3%AEteaux" class="mw-redirect" title="Cîteaux">Cîteaux</a> grew rapidly. Three years after entering, Bernard was sent with a group of twelve monks to found a new house at Vallée d'Absinthe, in the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Langres" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Langres">Diocese of Langres</a>. This Bernard named <i>Claire Vallée</i>, or <i>Clairvaux</i>, on 25 June 1115, and the names of Bernard and Clairvaux soon became inseparable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998129_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998129-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> During the absence of the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Langres" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Langres">Bishop of Langres</a>, Bernard was blessed as <a href="/wiki/Abbot" title="Abbot">abbot</a> by <a href="/wiki/William_of_Champeaux" title="William of Champeaux">William of Champeaux</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Ch%C3%A2lons-sur-Marne" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Châlons-sur-Marne">Bishop of Châlons-sur-Marne</a>. From then on a strong friendship grew between the abbot and the bishop, who was professor of theology at <a href="/wiki/Notre_Dame_of_Paris" class="mw-redirect" title="Notre Dame of Paris">Notre Dame of Paris</a> and the founder of <a href="/wiki/Abbey_of_St._Victor,_Paris" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbey of St. Victor, Paris">St. Victor Abbey in Paris</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The beginnings of <a href="/wiki/Clairvaux_Abbey" title="Clairvaux Abbey">Clairvaux Abbey</a> were austere; Bernard soon became ill. Nonetheless, candidates for the monastic life flocked to it in great numbers. Even his father and all his brothers entered Cîteaux, leaving only <a href="/wiki/Humbeline_of_Jully" title="Humbeline of Jully">Humbeline</a>, his sister, in the secular world. She, with the consent of her husband, later took the veil in the <a href="/wiki/Benedictine" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedictine">Benedictine</a> <a href="/wiki/Nunnery" class="mw-redirect" title="Nunnery">nunnery</a> of <a href="/wiki/Jully-les-Nonnains" class="mw-redirect" title="Jully-les-Nonnains">Jully-les-Nonnains</a>. <a href="/wiki/Gerard_of_Clairvaux_(died_1138)" title="Gerard of Clairvaux (died 1138)">Gerard of Clairvaux</a>, Bernard's older brother, became the <a href="/wiki/Obedientiaries" class="mw-redirect" title="Obedientiaries">cellarer</a> of Cîteaux. Clairvaux soon started founding new communities.<sup id="cite_ref-EiA_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EiA-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In 1118 <a href="/wiki/Trois-Fontaines_Abbey" title="Trois-Fontaines Abbey">Trois-Fontaines Abbey</a> was founded in the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Ch%C3%A2lons" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Châlons">diocese of Châlons</a>; in 1119 <a href="/wiki/Fontenay_Abbey" class="mw-redirect" title="Fontenay Abbey">Fontenay Abbey</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Autun" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Autun">Diocese of Autun</a>; and in 1121 <a href="/wiki/Foigny_Abbey" title="Foigny Abbey">Foigny Abbey</a> near <a href="/wiki/Vervins" title="Vervins">Vervins</a>.
</p><p>In addition to successes, Bernard also had his trials. During an absence from Clairvaux, the Grand Prior of the <a href="/wiki/Abbey_of_Cluny" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbey of Cluny">Abbey of Cluny</a> went to Clairvaux and enticed away Bernard's cousin, <a href="/wiki/Robert_of_Ch%C3%A2tillon" title="Robert of Châtillon">Robert of Châtillon</a>. This was the occasion of the longest and most emotional of Bernard's letters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dehio_212_Cluny.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Dehio_212_Cluny.jpg/220px-Dehio_212_Cluny.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="134" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Dehio_212_Cluny.jpg/330px-Dehio_212_Cluny.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Dehio_212_Cluny.jpg/440px-Dehio_212_Cluny.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3808" data-file-height="2316" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dehio_212_Cluny.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The abbey of Cluny as it would have looked in Bernard's time</div></div></div>
<p>The monks of the powerful Benedictine abbey of Cluny were unhappy to see Cîteaux take the lead role among the monastic orders. They criticized the Cistercian way of life. At the solicitation of <a href="/wiki/William_of_St-Thierry" title="William of St-Thierry">William of St.-Thierry</a>, Bernard defended the Cistercians with his <i>Apology</i>. <a href="/wiki/Peter_the_Venerable" title="Peter the Venerable">Peter the Venerable</a>, <a href="/wiki/Abbot_of_Cluny" title="Abbot of Cluny">abbot of Cluny</a>, answered Bernard and assured him of his admiration and friendship. In the meantime, Cluny launched a reform and <a href="/wiki/Abbot_Suger" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbot Suger">Abbot Suger</a>, the minister of <a href="/wiki/Louis_VI_of_France" title="Louis VI of France">Louis VI of France</a>, was converted by Bernard's <i>Apology</i>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Doctor_of_the_Church">Doctor of the Church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Doctor of the Church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Francisco_Ribalta_-_Christ_Embracing_St_Bernard_-_WGA19350.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Francisco_Ribalta_-_Christ_Embracing_St_Bernard_-_WGA19350.jpg/220px-Francisco_Ribalta_-_Christ_Embracing_St_Bernard_-_WGA19350.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="311" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Francisco_Ribalta_-_Christ_Embracing_St_Bernard_-_WGA19350.jpg/330px-Francisco_Ribalta_-_Christ_Embracing_St_Bernard_-_WGA19350.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Francisco_Ribalta_-_Christ_Embracing_St_Bernard_-_WGA19350.jpg/440px-Francisco_Ribalta_-_Christ_Embracing_St_Bernard_-_WGA19350.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="1131" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Francisco_Ribalta_-_Christ_Embracing_St_Bernard_-_WGA19350.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Christ Embracing St. Bernard by <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Ribalta" class="mw-redirect" title="Francisco Ribalta">Francisco Ribalta</a></div></div></div>
<p>In 1128, Bernard participated in the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Troyes_1129" class="mw-redirect" title="Council of Troyes 1129">Council of Troyes</a>, which had been convoked by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Honorius_II" title="Pope Honorius II">Pope Honorius II</a>, and was presided over by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Matthew_of_Albano" title="Matthew of Albano">Matthew of Albano</a>. The purpose of this council was to settle certain disputes of the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Paris" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Paris">bishops of Paris</a>, and regulate other matters of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Church of France">Church of France</a>. The bishops made Bernard secretary of the council, and charged him with drawing up the synodal statutes. After the council, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Verdun" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Verdun">bishop of Verdun</a> was deposed. It was at this council that Bernard composed a rule for the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Templar" title="Knights Templar">Knights Templar</a>; it soon became an ideal of Christian nobility. Around this time, he praised them in his <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la"><a href="/wiki/Liber_ad_milites_templi_de_laude_novae_militiae" title="Liber ad milites templi de laude novae militiae">Liber ad milites templi de laude novae militiae</a></i></span>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDurant1950593_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDurant1950593-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Schism">Schism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Schism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bernard's influence was soon felt in provincial affairs. He defended the rights of the Church against the encroachments of kings and princes, and recalled to their duty <a href="/wiki/Henri_Sanglier" title="Henri Sanglier">Henri Sanglier</a>, archbishop of Sens and <a href="/wiki/Stephen_of_Senlis" title="Stephen of Senlis">Stephen of Senlis</a>, bishop of Paris. When Honorius II died in 1130, a <a href="/wiki/Papal_schism_of_1130" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal schism of 1130">schism</a> broke out in the Church by the election of two popes, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_II" title="Pope Innocent II">Pope Innocent II</a> and <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Anacletus_II" title="Antipope Anacletus II">Antipope Anacletus II</a>. Innocent, having been banished from Rome by Anacletus, took refuge in France. King Louis VI convened a national council of the French bishops at <a href="/wiki/%C3%89tampes" title="Étampes">Étampes</a> and Bernard, summoned there by the bishops, was chosen to judge between the rival popes. He decided in favour of Innocent.
</p><p>Bernard travelled on to Italy and reconciled <a href="/wiki/Pisa" title="Pisa">Pisa</a> with <a href="/wiki/Genoa" title="Genoa">Genoa</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Milan" title="Milan">Milan</a> with the pope. The same year Bernard was again at the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Reims" title="Council of Reims">Council of Reims</a> at the side of Innocent II. He then went to <a href="/wiki/Aquitaine" title="Aquitaine">Aquitaine</a> where he succeeded for the time in detaching <a href="/wiki/William_X,_Duke_of_Aquitaine" title="William X, Duke of Aquitaine">William X, Duke of Aquitaine</a>, from the cause of Anacletus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998129_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998129-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Marten_Pepijn_-_Saint_Bernard_and_the_Duke_of_Aquitaine.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Marten_Pepijn_-_Saint_Bernard_and_the_Duke_of_Aquitaine.jpg/220px-Marten_Pepijn_-_Saint_Bernard_and_the_Duke_of_Aquitaine.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="302" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Marten_Pepijn_-_Saint_Bernard_and_the_Duke_of_Aquitaine.jpg/330px-Marten_Pepijn_-_Saint_Bernard_and_the_Duke_of_Aquitaine.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Marten_Pepijn_-_Saint_Bernard_and_the_Duke_of_Aquitaine.jpg/440px-Marten_Pepijn_-_Saint_Bernard_and_the_Duke_of_Aquitaine.jpg 2x" data-file-width="515" data-file-height="707" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Marten_Pepijn_-_Saint_Bernard_and_the_Duke_of_Aquitaine.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Saint Bernard and the Duke of Aquitaine</i>, by <a href="/wiki/Marten_Pepijn" title="Marten Pepijn">Marten Pepijn</a></div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> had decided to support Innocent through <a href="/wiki/Norbert_of_Xanten" title="Norbert of Xanten">Norbert of Xanten</a>, who was a friend of Bernard's. However, Innocent insisted on Bernard's company when he met with <a href="/wiki/Lothair_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Lothair II, Holy Roman Emperor">Lothair II, Holy Roman Emperor</a>. Lothair II became Innocent's strongest ally among the nobility. Although the councils of Étampes, <a href="/wiki/W%C3%BCrzburg" title="Würzburg">Würzburg</a>, <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Clermont" title="Council of Clermont">Clermont</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Rheims" class="mw-redirect" title="Rheims">Rheims</a> all supported Innocent, large portions of the Christian world still supported Anacletus.
</p><p>In a letter by Bernard to German Emperor Lothair regarding Antipope Anacletus, Bernard wrote, "It is a disgrace for Christ that a Jew sits on the throne of St. Peter's" and "Anacletus has not even a good reputation with his friends, while Innocent is illustrious beyond all doubt."
</p><p>Bernard wrote to Gerard of Angoulême (a letter known as Letter 126), which questioned Gerard's reasons for supporting Anacletus. Bernard later commented that Gerard was his most formidable opponent during the whole schism. After persuading Gerard, Bernard traveled to visit <a href="/wiki/William_X,_Duke_of_Aquitaine" title="William X, Duke of Aquitaine">William X, Duke of Aquitaine</a>. He was the hardest for Bernard to convince. He did not pledge allegiance to Innocent until 1135. After that, Bernard spent most of his time in <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a> persuading the Italians to pledge allegiance to Innocent. The conflict ended when Anacletus died in 1138.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECristiani1977_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECristiani1977-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1132, Bernard accompanied Innocent II into Italy, and at Cluny the pope abolished the dues which Clairvaux used to pay to that abbey. This action gave rise to a quarrel between the <a href="/wiki/Cistercians" title="Cistercians">White Monks</a> and the Black Monks which lasted 20 years. In May of that year, the pope, supported by the army of Lothair III, entered Rome, but Lothair III, feeling himself too weak to resist the partisans of Anacletus, retired beyond the Alps, and Innocent sought refuge in Pisa in September 1133. Bernard had returned to France in June and was continuing the work of peacemaking which he had commenced in 1130. Towards the end of 1134, he made a second journey into Aquitaine, where William X had relapsed into schism. Bernard invited William to the Mass which he celebrated in the Church of La Couldre. At the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a>, he "admonished the Duke not to despise God as he did His servants".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> William yielded and the schism ended. Bernard went again to Italy, where <a href="/wiki/Roger_II_of_Sicily" title="Roger II of Sicily">Roger II of Sicily</a> was endeavouring to withdraw the Pisans from their allegiance to Innocent. He recalled the city of Milan to obedience to the pope as they had followed the deposed <a href="/wiki/Anselm_V,_Archbishop_of_Milan" class="mw-redirect" title="Anselm V, Archbishop of Milan">Anselm V, Archbishop of Milan</a>. For this, he was offered, and he refused, the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Milan" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Milan">archbishopric of Milan</a>. He then returned to Clairvaux. Believing himself at last secure in his cloister, Bernard devoted himself to the composition of the works which won for him the title of "Doctor of the Church". He wrote at this time his sermons on the <a href="/wiki/Song_of_Songs" title="Song of Songs">Song of Songs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> In 1137, he was again forced to leave the abbey by order of the pope to put an end to the quarrel between Lothair and Roger of Sicily. At the conference held at Palermo, Bernard succeeded in convincing Roger of the rights of Innocent II. He also silenced the final supporters who sustained the schism. Anacletus died of "grief and disappointment" in 1138, and with him the schism ended.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1139, Bernard assisted at the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_the_Lateran" title="Second Council of the Lateran">Second Council of the Lateran</a>, in which the surviving adherents of the schism were definitively condemned. About the same time, Bernard was visited at Clairvaux by <a href="/wiki/Saint_Malachy" title="Saint Malachy">Malachy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Primate_of_All_Ireland" class="mw-redirect" title="Primate of All Ireland">Primate of All Ireland</a>, and a very close friendship formed between them. Malachy wanted to become a Cistercian, but the pope would not give his permission. Malachy died at Clairvaux in 1148.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflict_with_Abelard">Conflict with Abelard</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Conflict with Abelard">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Towards the close of the 11th century, a spirit of independence flourished within schools of <a href="/wiki/Philosophy" title="Philosophy">philosophy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a>. The movement found an ardent and powerful advocate in <a href="/wiki/Peter_Abelard" title="Peter Abelard">Peter Abelard</a>. Abelard's treatise on the <a href="/wiki/Trinity" title="Trinity">Trinity</a> had been condemned as heretical in 1121, and he was compelled to throw his own book into a fire. However, Abelard continued to develop his controversial teachings. Bernard is said to have held a meeting with Abelard intending to persuade him to amend his writings, during which Abelard repented and promised to do so. But once out of Bernard's presence, he reneged.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEvans2000115–123_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2000115–123-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Bernard then denounced Abelard to the pope and cardinals of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Curia" title="Roman Curia">Curia</a>. Abelard sought a debate with Bernard, but Bernard initially declined, saying he did not feel matters of such importance should be settled by logical analyses. Bernard's letters to William of St-Thierry also express his apprehension about confronting the preeminent logician. Abelard continued to press for a public debate, and made his challenge widely known, making it hard for Bernard to decline. In 1141, at the urgings of Abelard, the archbishop of Sens called a council of bishops, where Abelard and Bernard were to put their respective cases so Abelard would have a chance to clear his name.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEvans2000115–123_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2000115–123-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Bernard lobbied the prelates on the evening before the debate, swaying many of them to his view. The next day, after Bernard made his opening statement, Abelard decided to retire without attempting to answer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEvans2000115–123_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2000115–123-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The council found in favour of Bernard and their judgment was confirmed by the pope. Abelard submitted without resistance, and he retired to Cluny to live under the protection of Peter the Venerable, where he died two years later.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998129_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998129-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cistercian_Order_and_heresy">Cistercian Order and heresy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Cistercian Order and heresy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bernard had occupied himself in sending bands of monks from his overcrowded monastery into Germany, Sweden, England, Ireland, Portugal, Switzerland, and Italy. Some of these, at the command of Innocent II, took possession of Tre Fontane Abbey, from which Eugene III was chosen in 1145. Pope Innocent II died in the year 1143. His two successors, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Celestine_II" title="Pope Celestine II">Pope Celestine II</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pope_Lucius_II" title="Pope Lucius II">Pope Lucius II</a>, reigned only a short time, and then Bernard saw one of his disciples, Bernard of Pisa, and known thereafter as Eugene III, raised to the <a href="/wiki/Chair_of_Saint_Peter" title="Chair of Saint Peter">Chair of Saint Peter</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Bernard sent him, at the pope's own request, various instructions which comprise the <i>Book of Considerations,</i> the predominating idea of which is that the reformation of the Church ought to commence with the sanctity of the pope. Temporal matters are merely accessories; the principles according to Bernard's work were that piety and meditation were to precede action.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcManners1990210_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcManners1990210-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having previously helped end the schism within the Church, Bernard was now called upon to combat heresy. <a href="/wiki/Henry_of_Lausanne" title="Henry of Lausanne">Henry of Lausanne</a>, a former <a href="/wiki/Cluny_Abbey" title="Cluny Abbey">Cluniac</a> monk, had adopted the teachings of the Petrobrusians, followers of <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Bruys" title="Peter of Bruys">Peter of Bruys</a> and spread them in a modified form after Peter's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlphandéry1911298–299_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlphandéry1911298–299-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Henry of Lausanne's followers became known as Henricians. In June 1145, at the invitation of Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Alberic_of_Ostia" title="Alberic of Ostia">Alberic of Ostia</a>, Bernard traveled in southern France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcManners1990211_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcManners1990211-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> His preaching, aided by his ascetic looks and simple attire, helped doom the new sects. Both the Henrician and the Petrobrusian faiths began to die out by the end of that year. Soon afterwards, Henry of Lausanne was arrested, brought before the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Toulouse" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Toulouse">bishop of Toulouse</a>, and probably imprisoned for life. In a letter to the people of Toulouse, undoubtedly written at the end of 1146, Bernard calls upon them to extirpate the last remnants of the heresy. He also preached against <a href="/wiki/Catharism" title="Catharism">Catharism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Crusade_preaching">Crusade preaching</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Crusade preaching">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Second_Crusade_.281146.E2.80.9349.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_Crusade_(1146–49)">Second Crusade (1146–49)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Second Crusade (1146–49)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint-Bernard_pr%C3%AAchant_la_2e_croisade,_%C3%A0_V%C3%A9zelay,_en_1146.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Saint-Bernard_pr%C3%AAchant_la_2e_croisade%2C_%C3%A0_V%C3%A9zelay%2C_en_1146.jpg/220px-Saint-Bernard_pr%C3%AAchant_la_2e_croisade%2C_%C3%A0_V%C3%A9zelay%2C_en_1146.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="303" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Saint-Bernard_pr%C3%AAchant_la_2e_croisade%2C_%C3%A0_V%C3%A9zelay%2C_en_1146.jpg/330px-Saint-Bernard_pr%C3%AAchant_la_2e_croisade%2C_%C3%A0_V%C3%A9zelay%2C_en_1146.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Saint-Bernard_pr%C3%AAchant_la_2e_croisade%2C_%C3%A0_V%C3%A9zelay%2C_en_1146.jpg/440px-Saint-Bernard_pr%C3%AAchant_la_2e_croisade%2C_%C3%A0_V%C3%A9zelay%2C_en_1146.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1388" data-file-height="1911" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint-Bernard_pr%C3%AAchant_la_2e_croisade,_%C3%A0_V%C3%A9zelay,_en_1146.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Bernard preaching the second crusade in <a href="/wiki/V%C3%A9zelay" title="Vézelay">Vézelay</a> in 1146.</div></div></div>
<p>News came at this time from the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Land" title="Holy Land">Holy Land</a> that alarmed <a href="/wiki/Christendom" title="Christendom">Christendom</a>. Christians had been defeated at the <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Edessa_(1144)" title="Siege of Edessa (1144)">Siege of Edessa</a> and most of the county had fallen into the hands of the <a href="/wiki/Seljuq_dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Seljuq dynasty">Seljuk Turks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERiley-Smith199148_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERiley-Smith199148-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Jerusalem" title="Kingdom of Jerusalem">Kingdom of Jerusalem</a> and the other <a href="/wiki/Crusader_states" title="Crusader states">Crusader states</a> were threatened with similar disaster. Deputations of the bishops of <a href="/wiki/Armenian_Kingdom_of_Cilicia" title="Armenian Kingdom of Cilicia">Armenia</a> solicited aid from the pope, and the King of France also sent ambassadors. In 1144 Eugene III commissioned Bernard to preach the Second Crusade and granted the same <a href="/wiki/Indulgence" title="Indulgence">indulgences</a> for it which <a href="/wiki/Pope_Urban_II" title="Pope Urban II">Pope Urban II</a> had accorded to the <a href="/wiki/First_Crusade" title="First Crusade">First Crusade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDurant1950594_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDurant1950594-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There was at first virtually no popular enthusiasm for the crusade as there had been in 1095. Bernard found it expedient to dwell upon taking the cross as a potent means of gaining absolution for sin and attaining grace. On 31 March, with King <a href="/wiki/Louis_VII_of_France" title="Louis VII of France">Louis VII of France</a> present, he preached to an enormous crowd in a field at <a href="/wiki/V%C3%A9zelay" title="Vézelay">Vézelay</a>, making "the speech of his life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich2012_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich2012-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The full text has not survived, but a contemporary account says that "his voice rang out across the meadow like a celestial organ"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich2012_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich2012-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>James Meeker Ludlow describes the scene romantically in his book <i>The Age of the Crusades</i>:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>A large platform was erected on a hill outside the city. King and monk stood together, <a href="/wiki/Gelasian_doctrine" class="mw-redirect" title="Gelasian doctrine">representing the combined will of earth and heaven</a>. The enthusiasm of the assembly of Clermont in 1095, when <a href="/wiki/Peter_the_Hermit" title="Peter the Hermit">Peter the Hermit</a> and Urban II launched the first crusade, was matched by the holy fervor inspired by Bernard as he cried, "O ye who listen to me! Hasten to appease the anger of heaven, but no longer implore its goodness by vain complaints. Clothe yourselves in sackcloth, but also cover yourselves with your impenetrable <a href="/wiki/Buckler" title="Buckler">bucklers</a>. The din of arms, the danger, the labors, the fatigues of war, are the penances that God now imposes upon you. Hasten then to expiate your sins by victories over the Infidels, and let the deliverance of the holy places be the reward of your repentance." As in the olden scene, the cry "<i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la"><a href="/wiki/Deus_vult" title="Deus vult">Deus vult</a>! Deus vult</i></span>!</i><span style="white-space: nowrap;">&#8201;</span>" rolled over the fields, and was echoed by the voice of the orator: "Cursed be he who does not stain his sword with blood."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELudlow1896164–167_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELudlow1896164–167-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>When Bernard was finished the crowd enlisted en masse; they supposedly ran out of cloth to make crosses. Bernard is said to have flung off his own robe and began tearing it into strips to make more.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDurant1950594_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDurant1950594-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich2012_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich2012-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Others followed his example and he and his helpers were supposedly still producing crosses as night fell.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich2012_19-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich2012-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Unlike the First Crusade, the new venture attracted royalty, such as <a href="/wiki/Eleanor_of_Aquitaine" title="Eleanor of Aquitaine">Eleanor of Aquitaine, Queen of France</a>; <a href="/wiki/Thierry_of_Alsace" class="mw-redirect" title="Thierry of Alsace">Thierry of Alsace</a>, <a href="/wiki/Count_of_Flanders" title="Count of Flanders">Count of Flanders</a>; <a href="/wiki/Henry_I_of_Champagne" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry I of Champagne">Henry, the future Count of Champagne</a>; Louis's brother <a href="/wiki/Robert_I_of_Dreux" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert I of Dreux">Robert I of Dreux</a>; <a href="/wiki/Alphonse_I_of_Toulouse" class="mw-redirect" title="Alphonse I of Toulouse">Alphonse I of Toulouse</a>; <a href="/wiki/William_II_of_Nevers" class="mw-redirect" title="William II of Nevers">William II of Nevers</a>; <a href="/wiki/William_de_Warenne,_3rd_Earl_of_Surrey" title="William de Warenne, 3rd Earl of Surrey">William de Warenne, 3rd Earl of Surrey</a>; <a href="/wiki/Hugh_VII_of_Lusignan" title="Hugh VII of Lusignan">Hugh VII of Lusignan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Yves_II,_Count_of_Soissons" title="Yves II, Count of Soissons">Yves II, Count of Soissons</a>; and numerous other nobles and bishops. But an even greater show of support came from the common people. Bernard wrote to the pope a few days afterwards, "Cities and castles are now empty. There is not left one man to seven women, and everywhere there are widows to still-living husbands."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDurant1950594_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDurant1950594-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bernard then passed into Germany, and the reported miracles which multiplied almost at his every step undoubtedly contributed to the success of his mission. <a href="/wiki/Conrad_III_of_Germany" title="Conrad III of Germany">Conrad III of Germany</a> and his nephew <a href="/wiki/Frederick_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick I, Holy Roman Emperor">Frederick Barbarossa</a>, received the cross from the hand of Bernard.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERiley-Smith199148_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERiley-Smith199148-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Pope Eugenius came in person to France to encourage the enterprise. As in the First Crusade, the preaching led to attacks on <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a>; a fanatical French monk named <a href="/wiki/Radulf_the_Cistercian" title="Radulf the Cistercian">Radulphe</a> was apparently inspiring massacres of Jews in the Rhineland, <a href="/wiki/Cologne" title="Cologne">Cologne</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mainz" title="Mainz">Mainz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Worms,_Germany" title="Worms, Germany">Worms</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Speyer" title="Speyer">Speyer</a>, with Radulphe claiming Jews were not contributing financially to the rescue of the Holy Land. The <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Cologne" title="Archbishop of Cologne">archbishop of Cologne</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Mainz" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Mainz">archbishop of Mainz</a> were vehemently opposed to these attacks and asked Bernard to denounce them. This he did, but when the campaign continued, Bernard traveled from Flanders to Germany to deal with the problems in person. He then found Radulphe in Mainz and was able to silence him, returning him to his monastery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDurant1950391_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDurant1950391-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The last years of Bernard's life were saddened by the failure of the Second Crusade he had preached, the entire responsibility for which was thrown upon him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130_13-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Bernard considered it his duty to send an apology to the Pope and it is inserted in the second part of his <i>"Book of Considerations."</i> There he explains how the sins of the crusaders were the cause of their misfortune and failures.
</p>
<h3><span id="Wendish_Crusade_.281147.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Wendish_Crusade_(1147)">Wendish Crusade (1147)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Wendish Crusade (1147)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bernard preached the <a href="/wiki/Wendish_Crusade" title="Wendish Crusade">Wendish Crusade</a> against Western Slavs, setting a goal to the crusade of battling them "until such a time as, by God's help, they shall either be converted or deleted".<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Final_years_.281149.E2.80.9353.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years_(1149–53)">Final years (1149–53)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Final years (1149–53)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:BernhardClairvaux_Lactatio_SourceUnknown.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/BernhardClairvaux_Lactatio_SourceUnknown.jpg/220px-BernhardClairvaux_Lactatio_SourceUnknown.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="356" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c7/BernhardClairvaux_Lactatio_SourceUnknown.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="250" data-file-height="404" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:BernhardClairvaux_Lactatio_SourceUnknown.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Bernard receiving milk from the breast of the Virgin Mary. The scene is a legend which allegedly took place at <a href="/wiki/Speyer_Cathedral" title="Speyer Cathedral">Speyer Cathedral</a> in 1146.</div></div></div>
<p>The death of his contemporaries served as a warning to Bernard of his own approaching end. The first to die was Suger in 1152, of whom Bernard wrote to Eugene III, "If there is any precious vase adorning the palace of the King of Kings it is the soul of the venerable Suger". Conrad III and his son Henry died the same year. Bernard died at age sixty-three on 20 August 1153, after forty years of monastic life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130_13-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> He was buried at Clairvaux Abbey, and after its dissolution in 1792 by the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolution" title="French Revolution">French revolutionary government</a> his remains were transferred to <a href="/wiki/Troyes_Cathedral" title="Troyes Cathedral">Troyes Cathedral</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Doctor_Mellifluus" title="Doctor Mellifluus">Doctor Mellifluus</a></div>
<p>Bernard was named a Doctor of the Church in 1830. At the 800th anniversary of his death, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> issued an encyclical about him, titled <i><a href="/wiki/Doctor_Mellifluus" title="Doctor Mellifluus">Doctor Mellifluus</a></i>, in which he labeled him "The Last of the Fathers." The central elements of Bernard's Mariology are how he explained the <a href="/wiki/Virginity" title="Virginity">virginity</a> of Mary, <i>the "Star of the Sea"</i>, and her role as <a href="/wiki/Mediatrix" title="Mediatrix">Mediatrix</a>.
</p><p>The first abbot of Clairvaux developed a rich theology of sacred space and <a href="/wiki/Cistercian_Hymnal" title="Cistercian Hymnal">music</a>, writing extensively on both.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/John_Calvin" title="John Calvin">John Calvin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a> quoted Bernard several times<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELane1999100_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELane1999100-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> in support of the doctrine of <i><a href="/wiki/Sola_Fide" class="mw-redirect" title="Sola Fide">Sola Fide</a>.</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalvin1960bk.3_ch.2_§25,_bk.3_ch.12_§3_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalvin1960bk.3_ch.2_§25,_bk.3_ch.12_§3-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuther1930130_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuther1930130-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Calvin also quotes him in setting forth his doctrine of a forensic alien righteousness, or as it is commonly called <a href="/wiki/Imputed_righteousness" title="Imputed righteousness">imputed righteousness</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalvin1960bk.3_ch.11_§22,_bk.3_ch.25_§2_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalvin1960bk.3_ch.11_§22,_bk.3_ch.25_§2-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Spirituality">Spirituality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Spirituality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Stained_glass_St_Bernard_MNMA_Cl3273.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Stained_glass_St_Bernard_MNMA_Cl3273.jpg/220px-Stained_glass_St_Bernard_MNMA_Cl3273.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="333" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Stained_glass_St_Bernard_MNMA_Cl3273.jpg/330px-Stained_glass_St_Bernard_MNMA_Cl3273.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Stained_glass_St_Bernard_MNMA_Cl3273.jpg/440px-Stained_glass_St_Bernard_MNMA_Cl3273.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1620" data-file-height="2450" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Stained_glass_St_Bernard_MNMA_Cl3273.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Stained glass representing Bernard. Upper Rhine, c. 1450</div></div></div>
<p>Bernard was instrumental in re-emphasizing the importance of <i><a href="/wiki/Lectio_divina" class="mw-redirect" title="Lectio divina">lectio divina</a></i> and contemplation for monks. Bernard had observed that when <i>lectio divina</i> was neglected, monasticism suffered.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunninghamEgan199691–92_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunninghamEgan199691–92-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Bernard "noted centuries ago: the people who are their own <a href="/wiki/Spiritual_director" class="mw-redirect" title="Spiritual director">spiritual directors</a> have fools for disciples."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunninghamEgan199621_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunninghamEgan199621-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bernard's theology and <a href="/wiki/Mariology(RC)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mariology(RC)">Mariology</a> continue to be of major importance, particularly within the Cistercian and Trappist Orders.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> Bernard helped found 163 monasteries in different parts of Europe. His influence led Alexander III to launch reforms that led to the establishment of <a href="/wiki/Canon_law_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon law (Catholic Church)">canon law</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuffy1997101_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuffy1997101-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> by Alexander III 18 January 1174.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKemp194513–28_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKemp194513–28-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> He is labeled the "Mellifluous Doctor" for his eloquence. Cistercians honour him as one of the greatest early Cistercians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130_13-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson1998130-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His feast day (observed in several denominations) is 20 August.
</p><p>Bernard is <a href="/wiki/Dante_Alighieri" title="Dante Alighieri">Dante Alighieri</a>'s last guide, in <i><a href="/wiki/Divine_Comedy" title="Divine Comedy">Divine Comedy</a></i>, as he travels through the <a href="/wiki/Empyrean" title="Empyrean">Empyrean</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Dante's choice appears to be based on Bernard's contemplative mysticism, his devotion to Mary, and his reputation for eloquence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBotterill1994_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBotterill1994-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Couvent_et_Basilique_Saint-Bernard" title="Couvent et Basilique Saint-Bernard">Couvent et Basilique Saint-Bernard</a>, a collection of buildings dating from the 12th, 17th and 19th centuries, is dedicated to Bernard and stands in his birthplace of <a href="/wiki/Fontaine-l%C3%A8s-Dijon" title="Fontaine-lès-Dijon">Fontaine-lès-Dijon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-culture.gouv.fr_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-culture.gouv.fr-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Hymns">Hymns</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Hymns">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bernard of Clairvaux is the attributed author of poems often translated in English hymnals as:
</p>
<ul><li>"<a href="/wiki/O_Sacred_Head,_Now_Wounded" title="O Sacred Head, Now Wounded">O Sacred Head, Now Wounded</a>"</li>
<li>"Jesus the Very Thought of Thee"</li>
<li>"Jesus, Thou Joy of Loving Hearts"</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:StBernardFS.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/StBernardFS.jpg/220px-StBernardFS.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="290" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/StBernardFS.jpg/330px-StBernardFS.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/StBernardFS.jpg/440px-StBernardFS.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2276" data-file-height="3000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:StBernardFS.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>An <a href="/wiki/Engraving" title="Engraving">engraving</a> of The <a href="/wiki/Lactation_of_Saint_Bernard" class="mw-redirect" title="Lactation of Saint Bernard">Lactation of Saint Bernard</a>. The Virgin Mary is shooting milk into the eye of Saint Bernard of Clairvaux from her right breast.</div></div></div>
<p>The modern critical edition is <i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Sancti Bernardi opera</i></span></i> (1957–1977), edited by <a href="/wiki/Jean_Leclercq_(monk)" title="Jean Leclercq (monk)">Jean Leclercq</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESBOp_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESBOp-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bernard's works include:
</p>
<ul><li><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-code{color:inherit;background:inherit;border:none;padding:inherit}.mw-parser-output .cs1-hidden-error{display:none;color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-visible-error{color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{display:none;color:#3a3;margin-left:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-format{font-size:95%}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-left{padding-left:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-right{padding-right:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .citation .mw-selflink{font-weight:inherit}</style><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvauxc._1120" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><i>De gradibus humilitatis et superbiae</i> &#91;<i>The steps of humility and pride</i>&#93; (in Latin). c. 1120.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=De+gradibus+humilitatis+et+superbiae&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._939–972c_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._939–972c-37">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvaux" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><a href="/wiki/Apologia_ad_Guillelmum" title="Apologia ad Guillelmum"><i>Apologia ad Guillelmum Sancti Theoderici Abbatem</i></a> &#91;<i>Apology to William of St. Thierry</i>&#93; (in Latin).</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Apologia+ad+Guillelmum+Sancti+Theoderici+Abbatem&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span> Written in the defence of the Cistercians against the claims of the monks of Cluny.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._893–918a_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._893–918a-38">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvaux1122" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><i>De conversione ad clericos sermo seu liber</i> &#91;<i>On the conversion of clerics</i>&#93; (in Latin). 1122.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=De+conversione+ad+clericos+sermo+seu+liber&amp;rft.date=1122&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._833–856d_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._833–856d-39">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvauxc._1128" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><i>De gratia et libero arbitrio</i> &#91;<i>On grace and free choice</i>&#93; (in Latin). c. 1128.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=De+gratia+et+libero+arbitrio&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._999–1030a_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._999–1030a-40">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvaux" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><i>De diligendo Dei</i> &#91;<i>On loving God</i>&#93; (in Latin).</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=De+diligendo+Dei&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._971–1000b_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._971–1000b-41">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvaux1129" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><a href="/wiki/Liber_ad_milites_templi_de_laude_novae_militiae" title="Liber ad milites templi de laude novae militiae"><i>Liber ad milites templi de laude novae militiae</i></a> &#91;<i>In Praise of the new knighthood</i>&#93; (in Latin). 1129.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Liber+ad+milites+templi+de+laude+novae+militiae&amp;rft.date=1129&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._917–940b_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._917–940b-42">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvauxc._1144" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><i>De praecepto et dispensatione libri</i> &#91;<i>Book of precepts and dispensations</i>&#93; (in Latin). c. 1144.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=De+praecepto+et+dispensatione+libri&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._857–894c_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._857–894c-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvauxc._1150" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><i>De consideratione</i> &#91;<i>On consideration</i>&#93; (in Latin). c. 1150.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=De+consideratione&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span> Addressed to Pope Eugene III.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._727–808a_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._727–808a-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvaux" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><i>Liber De vita et rebus gestis Sancti Malachiae Hiberniae Episcopi</i> &#91;<i>The life and death of Saint Malachy, bishop of Ireland</i>&#93; (in Latin).</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Liber+De+vita+et+rebus+gestis+Sancti+Malachiae+Hiberniae+Episcopi&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span> <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._1073–1118a_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL182,_cols._1073–1118a-45">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBernard_of_Clairvaux" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><i>De moribus et officio episcoporum</i> (in Latin).</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=De+moribus+et+officio+episcoporum&amp;rft.au=Bernard+of+Clairvaux&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span> A letter to Henri Sanglier, Archbishop of Sens on the duties of bishops.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>His sermons are also numerous:
</p>
<ul><li>Most famous are his <i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Sermones super Cantica Canticorum</i></span></i> (<i>Sermons on the Song of Songs</i>). Although it has at times been suggested that the sermon form is a rhetorical device in a set of works which were only ever designed to be read, since such finely polished and lengthy literary pieces could not accurately have been recorded by a monk while Bernard was preaching, recent scholarship has tended toward the theory that, although what exists in these texts was certainly the product of Bernard's writing, they likely found their origins in sermons preached to the monks of Clairvaux.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> Bernard began to write these in 1135 but died without completing the series, with 86 sermons complete. These sermons contain an autobiographical passage, sermon 26, mourning the death of his brother, Gerard.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVerbaal2004_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVerbaal2004-48">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL183,_cols._785–1198A_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL183,_cols._785–1198A-49">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> After Bernard died, the English Cistercian <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_of_Hoyland" title="Gilbert of Hoyland">Gilbert of Hoyland</a> continued Bernard's incomplete series of 86 sermons on the biblical Song of Songs. Gilbert wrote 47 sermons before he died in 1172, taking the series up to Chapter 5 of the Song of Songs. Another English Cistercian abbot, <a href="/wiki/John_of_Ford" title="John of Ford">John of Ford</a>, wrote another 120 sermons on the Song of Songs, so completing the Cistercian sermon-commentary on the book.</li>
<li>There are 125 surviving <i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Sermones per annum</i></span></i> (<i>Sermons on the Liturgical Year</i>).</li>
<li>There are also the <i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Sermones de diversis</i></span></i> (<i>Sermons on Different Topics</i>).</li>
<li>547 letters survive.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESBOpv._7–8_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESBOpv._7–8-50">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>Many letters, treatises, and other works, falsely attributed to him survive, and are now referred to as works by pseudo-Bernard.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> These include:
</p>
<ul><li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFpseudo-Bernard_(pseud._of_Guigo_I)c._1150" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><a href="/wiki/Guigo_I" title="Guigo I">pseudo-Bernard (pseud. of Guigo I)</a> (c. 1150). <i>L'échelle du cloître</i> &#91;<i>The scale of the cloister</i>&#93; (letter) (in French).</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=L%27%C3%A9chelle+du+clo%C3%AEtre&amp;rft.au=pseudo-Bernard+%28pseud.+of+Guigo+I%29&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFpseudo-Bernard" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source">pseudo-Bernard. <i>Meditatio</i> &#91;<i>Meditations</i>&#93; (in Latin).</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Meditatio&amp;rft.au=pseudo-Bernard&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span> This was probably written at some point in the thirteenth century. It circulated extensively in the Middle Ages under Bernard's name and was one of the most popular religious works of the later Middle Ages. Its theme is self-knowledge as the beginning of wisdom; it begins with the phrase "Many know much, but do not know themselves".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPL184,_cols._485–508_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPL184,_cols._485–508-51">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBestul2012164_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBestul2012164-52">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFpseudo-Bernard" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source">pseudo-Bernard. <i>L'édification de la maison intérieure</i> (in French).</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=L%27%C3%A9dification+de+la+maison+int%C3%A9rieure&amp;rft.au=pseudo-Bernard&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABernard+of+Clairvaux" class="Z3988"></span><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGildas1907_4-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGildas1907-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>

<li>Conrad Rudolph, <i>The 'Things of Greater Importance': Bernard of Clairvaux's Apologia and the Medieval Attitude Toward Art</i>, (Philadelphia: University of Pennsylvania Press, 1990) [Includes the <i>Apologia</i> in both Leclercq's Latin text and English translation]</li>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-3">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_de_Montbard" title="André de Montbard">André de Montbard</a>, one of the founders of the Knights Templar, was a half-brother of Bernard's mother.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-11"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-11">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Other mystics such as <a href="/wiki/John_of_the_Cross" title="John of the Cross">John of the Cross</a> also found their language and symbols in Song of Songs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunninghamEgan1996128_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunninghamEgan1996128-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-29"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-29">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">His texts are prescribed readings in Cistercian congregations.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-36"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-36">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">For a research guide see <a href="#CITEREFMcGuire2013">McGuire (2013)</a>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-47"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-47">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">For a history of the debate over the <i>Sermons</i>, and an attempted solution, see <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><span id="CITEREFLeclercq1976" class="citation">Leclercq, Jean. Introduction. In <a href="#CITEREFWalsh1976">Walsh (1976)</a>, pp.&#160;vii–xxx.</span></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="External_links">External links</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard_of_Clairvaux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: External links">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
